# Book map: G. Strang, E. Herman et al., *Calculus*, Volumes 1–3, OpenStax, 2016

Licence of the book: CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 ([source](https://openstax.org/details/books/calculus-volume-1)). This map holds item numbers, page numbers and our own one-line paraphrases, never the book's text.

Generated from the master checklist of the topic, [`docs/coverage/calculus/checklist.md`](../coverage/calculus/checklist.md) (one row per result, shared by the books of the topic; its notes say what Mathlib has); `lake build Calculus` builds the topic. Items are in the book's order.

1327 item entries, 690 checklist rows: 351 proved here, 272 in Mathlib, 0 partly in Mathlib, 0 not formalized, 67 not a result.

*Proved here*: proved in this library (the Lean names are `UndergradKB.` declarations). *In Mathlib*: Mathlib has the result; the Lean names are Mathlib's, followed by the course-layer theorem of this library that states it in the book's terms, when there is one. *Partly in Mathlib*: only part of the item is formalized (the checklist note says which part). *Not formalized*: a gap. *Not a result*: a definition, notation or remark.

| 1:§1.1 Definition (function) p16 | CA-001 | not a result | – | A function from a set X to a set Y assigns to each input in X exactly one output in Y; the inputs form the domain and the outputs attained form the range. |
| 1:§1.1 Zeros and intercepts p22 | CA-002 | not a result | – | A zero of f is an input x with f(x)=0; the x-intercepts of the graph are the points (x,0) at the zeros, and the y-intercept is (0,f(0)). |

| 2:Definition (second derivative as derivative of the first) p.563 | CA-393 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_second_deriv, HasDerivAt.of_local_left_inverse, HasDerivAt.comp | The second derivative of a parametric curve is $d^2y/dx^2=\dfrac{\frac{d}{dt}(dy/dx)}{dx/dt}$. |

| 2:Theorem 7.4 p.575 | CA-400 | in Mathlib | Complex.norm_mul_cos_arg, Complex.norm_mul_sin_arg, Complex.tan_arg, polarCoord, Complex.sq_norm; course layer: UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rectangular_x_eq_r_cos; UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rectangular_y_eq_r_sin; UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_radius_sq; UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.tan_polar_angle | Polar and rectangular coordinates are related by $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$ and $r^2=x^2+y^2$, $\tan\theta=y/x$ (for x nonzero). |

| 2:Theorem 7.5 p.586 | CA-406 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_axis, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_pole, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_vertical, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_curve_symmetry | Symmetry tests for polar curves: about the polar axis if (r,theta) in graph implies (r,-theta) (or (-r,pi-theta)); about the pole if (r,theta) implies (-r,theta) (or (r,theta+pi)); about the vertical line theta=pi/2 if (r,theta) implies (r,pi-theta) (or (-r,-theta)). |

| 3:§7.4 (uniqueness of power series, used in Example 7.25) p.793 | CA-388 | in Mathlib | HasFPowerSeriesAt.eq_formalMultilinearSeries, HasFPowerSeriesAt.eq_zero; course layer: UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.power_series_coefficients_unique; UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.power_series_zero_coefficients | If two power series agree as functions near a point, their coefficients are equal; hence in a power-series solution each coefficient of x^n must vanish separately. |
